Just yesterday, we were supposed to go to the hardware store for some new hinges for the back gate. The old ones are rusting terribly, you know, because of all that rain I mentioned. We’ve had the worst luck with rust on that side of the house. I think it’s because the gutters over there always overflow when it pours. Arthur’s been meaning to clean them out since October. \n\nBut anyway, what was it? Oh, yes, the plans. He says he always has to check behind me now to make sure I’ve actually written things down or finished what I said I would. And the other volunteers at the center are starting to do the same thing. It makes me feel awful, really, like I'm a burden, but I just... I get so easily sidetracked.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Well, act on impulse? I don't know if I'd say that, exactly. I mean, I don't go out and buy a new car on a whim or anything like that. Though, speaking of cars, Arthur did just get the oil changed in the Buick. He always takes it to that little shop on Elm Street. The mechanic there, Joe, he’s such a nice young man. He always asks about my granddaughter. He actually went to high school with her mother, my daughter Susan. Susan was a cheerleader, you know, and she had the most beautiful uniform. It was blue and gold, and I had to wash it by hand every Sunday night so it would be ready for the game on Monday. We used to use that special soap... oh, what was it called? It came in a pink box. \n\nBut anyway, impulse... no, I don't think I do things without thinking. I just get distracted, like I said. I might start something and then see something else that needs doing, but I don't think I'm impulsive. Just... busy, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus and completing tasks are affecting your relationships with friends or your role in the community?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was mentioning just a minute ago, yes, it really is. It's causing a real strain, both at home with Arthur and over at the community center. The other volunteers—especially Martha and Susan... oh, not my daughter Susan, a different Susan. Susan Miller. She makes the most wonderful peach cobbler for our charity bake sales. We had a sale just last month to raise money for the new library books, and her cobbler sold out in ten minutes! I tried to get the recipe from her, but she says it's a strict family secret passed down from her great-grandmother who lived in Georgia. They really do grow the best peaches down in Georgia, don't they? Arthur and I took a road trip down there back in '98... or maybe it was '99? We drove the whole way in that old station wagon we used to have. \n\nBut anyway, what was I... oh, right, the center. Yes, Susan and the other ladies, they feel like they just can't count on me to keep our plans straight anymore. They're always having to check behind me now, just like Arthur does, to make sure I actually finished setting up the tables or calling the folks I was supposed to call. So yes, it’s definitely affecting my role there. It makes me feel just awful that they have to baby me like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Well, restless... I suppose you could say that. Arthur always tells me I'm like a little motor that won't quite shut off. Especially when we're just sitting in the evenings watching television. We like to watch Jeopardy and Wheel of Fortune right after dinner. Pat Sajak, you know, I heard he was retiring. Or maybe he already did? Vanna White is still there, though, bless her. She wears the most gorgeous gowns. I actually tried to sew a dress like one of hers for my niece's wedding years ago—oh, what a disaster that was. The silk just kept slipping right under the presser foot of my old Singer machine. I’ve had that sewing machine since 1974, can you believe it? My mother bought it for me at Sears. \n\nBut what was I... oh, sitting still. Yes. When I’m watching television, or even if I'm just sitting in a meeting for the neighborhood watch, I just can't seem to stay entirely still. I’m usually tapping my foot on the rug, or rubbing my fingers together, or just fussing with whatever happens to be in my lap. A napkin, the remote control, the buttons on my cardigan... I just always seem to be fiddling with something. I don't even realize I'm doing it half the time until Arthur reaches over and pats my hand to make me stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself leaving your seat when you're supposed to be sitting still?\n\nPatient: Leave my seat? Oh, heavens no. I don't just get up and wander off when I'm supposed to be sitting. Well, unless I'm at home and remember I left the kettle on, but that's different. Now, when we're at church on Sunday mornings, for instance... Pastor Thomas has been giving these very long sermons lately. He’s a wonderful speaker, truly he is, but he does tend to go on a bit. His wife, Mary, she plays the organ, and she has the most beautiful singing voice. She actually sang at my niece's wedding—the same one I tried to make that silk dress for! It was an outdoor wedding, out by the lake, and the weather was just perfect. Not a single cloud in the sky. We were all so worried it would rain because it had just poured the entire week before, and you know how mud completely ruins a good pair of heels. I had these lovely navy blue pumps that I bought at Macy's... oh, they must be twenty years old now, but they still look brand new because I only wear them on special occasions. \n\nBut what were we... oh, getting up. No, no, I stay right there in my pew. I might be tapping my foot or fixing the pleats in my skirt, like I mentioned before, but I know better than to just stand up and walk out in the middle of a service or a meeting. I just stay put until it's time to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties are affecting your overall well-being or happiness?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I mean, nobody likes feeling like they can't keep their own life straight, do they? It does get me down sometimes. Arthur tells me I shouldn't fret so much, but he's always been the easygoing one. He's got this old armchair in the den, the brown leather one, and he can just sit there for hours reading his historical biographies. He's reading one right now about Abraham Lincoln. Did you know Lincoln kept important letters in his stovepipe hat? I always thought that was just a story they told children in school, but Arthur says it's right there in the book. It makes you wonder how he kept them from getting all sweaty in the summer, doesn't it? Summers back then must have been dreadful without air conditioning. We had our AC go out two years ago right in the middle of August, and it took the repairman three days to come out. Three days! We had fans running in every room, but it just blew the hot air around like a convection oven. \n\nBut anyway... what was I saying? Oh, my happiness. I don't know if I'd say it's affecting my overall happiness, exactly. I have a good life, a wonderful husband, beautiful grandchildren... I just wish I didn't feel so scattered all the time. It's frustrating, more than anything. Like I'm always trying to catch up to myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness at work or when you're alone, or are they mostly happening at home or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Well, since I'm retired, I don't really have a job to go to anymore. I used to work as a receptionist at a dental office, Dr. Peterson's office, right off Main Street. He was such a gentle dentist, never made you feel a thing when he gave those novocaine shots. He retired to Florida, Naples I think, or maybe it was Fort Myers. They get terrible hurricanes down there, don't they? I always worry about my sister, she lives near Tampa. She has this beautiful little lanai where she grows orchids, just stunning things. I tried to grow an orchid once, but I think I overwatered it. The leaves went all yellow and mushy. They say you're only supposed to give them an ice cube a week, which just sounds so strange for a tropical plant, doesn't it? Ice cubes in the jungle! \n\nBut... oh, where it happens. Yes. It's mostly at home, and over at the community center where I volunteer. It's actually started causing some real strain, to be honest. Arthur gets so frustrated with me, and the other ladies at the center... well, they just can't count on me to keep the plans straight anymore. If I'm supposed to organize the bake sale or keep track of the donations, someone always has to check behind me to make sure I didn't mix up the dates or lose the sign-up sheet. It's embarrassing, really. I just can't seem to keep it all organized in my head without someone looking over my shoulder.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness when you're alone at home?\n\nPatient: When I'm alone? Oh, well... I suppose I haven't really paid much attention to that. When Arthur is out playing golf with his buddies—they play every Tuesday and Thursday morning, weather permitting, of course. They play at that course over by the new shopping center. The one with the big Target? I went in there the other day just to pick up some new hand towels for the guest bathroom, because the old ones were getting so frayed around the edges. I don't know why they make things so poorly these days. You used to buy a towel and it would last you thirty years! Anyway, I walked out of there with three new throw pillows, a bag of potting soil, and completely forgot the towels. \n\nBut... what was I saying? Oh, being alone. I don't know, really. I guess I'm always doing something, but I couldn't tell you if I'm more restless or not when nobody else is around. I just sort of putter about the house.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they are speaking?\n\nPatient: Well, Arthur certainly thinks I do. He's always telling me I cut him off before he finishes his sentences. It drives him up the wall, especially when he's trying to tell one of his long golf stories. He has this one story about getting a hole-in-one on the 14th hole back in 1998, and he tells it with all these dramatic pauses. The 14th hole at that course has this awful water hazard right in front of the green, just a magnet for golf balls. My brother-in-law, Frank, lost three balls in that same pond on Thanksgiving morning once. We had to hold dinner for an hour because he refused to leave until he fished them out with that little scooper thing on a stick. By the time he got back, the turkey was practically dry as a bone. I had to make extra gravy just to save the meal, and you know how tricky gravy can be. You have to whisk it constantly or it gets all lumpy, and nobody likes lumpy gravy. \n\nBut... oh, interrupting. Yes, I do the same thing at the community center during our group planning meetings for the bake sales and things. I just... I get so afraid I'll lose the thought if I don't say it right then and there. It's like my brain is a leaky bucket, and if I don't pour the idea out immediately, it just drips away before I can share it. So I just blurt it out, even if someone else is talking. I don't mean to be rude, really I don't.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blur out answers before the questions have been completed when you're in a conversation?\n\nPatient: Blurting out answers? Oh, I don't know if I do that exactly. I mean, I do interrupt, like I said with Arthur and his golf stories. He really does take forever to get to the point. His brother Frank is the exact same way. They both get that from their mother, rest her soul. She could talk for forty-five minutes about the price of a head of lettuce at the Piggly Wiggly. She used to make this wonderful iceberg wedge salad with homemade blue cheese dressing. You can't find good blue cheese anymore, it's all that bottled stuff that tastes like vinegar and sadness. I tried making it from scratch once, but the cheese got all crumbly and wouldn't mix right with the mayonnaise. You really need a good quality mayonnaise for that, none of that low-fat nonsense. \n\nBut answering before someone finishes asking... I don't really think I do that. At least, nobody has ever pointed it out to me like they do with the interrupting. I just try to wait until they ask, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these feelings of restlessness and difficulty focusing when you're in quiet, low-stimulation environments, like when you're reading a book or taking a walk alone?\n\nPatient: Quiet things? Oh, I suppose they are rather difficult for me. Even when I try to do something peaceful, like my knitting... I joined a knitting circle at the library on Wednesday afternoons. They have this lovely room with big windows that look out over the community garden. The tomatoes they grew last summer were just spectacular. I tried growing tomatoes once, those little cherry ones, but the squirrels got to every single one of them before they even turned red. They're such menaces, those squirrels. They dug up all my tulip bulbs last fall, too. I had ordered these special black tulips from a catalog—well, they call them black, but they're really a very deep purple. They would have looked so striking next to the yellow daffodils. \n\nBut what was I... oh, the knitting. Yes, even when I'm just sitting there trying to knit quietly, I can't seem to just *be* quiet. I end up chatting with the woman next to me, or I start humming along to whatever tune is stuck in my head. Or I'll suddenly realize I need a different size needle and I have to get up and dig through my bag, and then I'll decide to organize my yarn while I'm at it. I just can't seem to sit calmly and do the quiet thing, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Waiting my turn? Oh, heavens, yes. It's dreadful. Like waiting in line at the pharmacy... I just get so fidgety, shifting from one foot to the other. The pharmacy down on Elm Street is always so backed up on Tuesday mornings. That's when they get their deliveries, you see, and the pharmacist—a lovely young man named David, he always wears these brightly colored bow ties—he gets so flustered trying to sort the new stock and help the customers. His wife just had a baby, a little girl. They named her Eleanor. Such a classic name, isn't it? Much better than these modern names people make up nowadays. My neighbor's daughter just named her baby "Riot." Can you imagine? A little boy named Riot. I suppose it's fitting, though, he cries constantly. I can hear him through the living room wall when I'm trying to watch my afternoon programs. 

But... oh, waiting. Yes. Whether it's the pharmacy line or waiting my turn to speak at those community center meetings, I just can't stand it. I catch myself sighing loudly, or I end up just jumping in too soon because I simply can't hold it in any longer. It's terribly impatient of me, I know.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, hello. Thank you for seeing me. I suppose... well, my family thought it would be a good idea for me to come in and talk to someone. I just never seem to settle down and finish one project before I'm up and on to the next one.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself struggling to complete tasks because you get distracted or lose focus?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. Like with the church bulletin or when we have our planning meetings for the garden club. We’ve had the worst luck with rust on that side of the house. I think it’s because the gutters over there always overflow when it pours. She makes the most wonderful peach cobbler for our charity bake sales. We had a sale just last month to raise money for the new library books, and her cobbler sold out in ten minutes! I tried to get the recipe from her, but she says it's a strict family secret passed down from her great-grandmother who lived in Georgia. They really do grow the best peaches down in Georgia, don't they? Arthur and I took a road trip down there back in '98... or maybe it was '99? We drove the whole way in that old station wagon we used to have. \n\nBut anyway, what was I... oh, right, the center. Yes, Susan and the other ladies, they feel like they just can't count on me to keep our plans straight anymore. They're always having to check behind me now, just like Arthur does, to make sure I actually finished setting up the tables or calling the folks I was supposed to call. So yes, it’s definitely affecting my role there. It makes me feel just awful that they have to baby me like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Well, restless... I suppose you could say that. Arthur always tells me I'm like a little motor that won't quite shut off. Especially when we're just sitting in the evenings watching television. We like to watch Jeopardy and Wheel of Fortune right after dinner. Pat Sajak, you know, I heard he was retiring. Or maybe he already did? Vanna White is still there, though, bless her. She wears the most gorgeous gowns. I actually tried to sew a dress like one of hers for my niece's wedding years ago—oh, what a disaster that was. The silk just kept slipping right under the presser foot of my old Singer machine. I’ve had that sewing machine since 1974, can you believe it? My mother bought it for me at Sears. \n\nBut what was I... oh, sitting still. Yes. When I’m watching television, or even if I'm just sitting in a meeting for the neighborhood watch, I just can't seem to stay entirely still. I’m usually tapping my foot on the rug, or rubbing my fingers together, or just fussing with whatever happens to be in my lap. A napkin, the remote control, the buttons on my cardigan... I just always seem to be fiddling with something. I don't even realize I'm doing it half the time until Arthur reaches over and pats my hand to make me stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself leaving your seat when you're supposed to be sitting still?\n\nPatient: Leave my seat? Oh, heavens no. I don't just get up and wander off when I'm supposed to be sitting. Well, unless I'm at home and remember I left the kettle on, but that's different. Now, when we're at church on Sunday mornings, for instance... Pastor Thomas has been giving these very long sermons lately. He’s a wonderful speaker, truly he is, but he does tend to go on a bit. His wife, Mary, she plays the organ, and she has the most beautiful singing voice. She actually sang at my niece's wedding—the same one I tried to make that silk dress for! It was an outdoor wedding, out by the lake, and the weather was just perfect. Not a single cloud in the sky. We were all so worried it would rain because it had just poured the entire week before, and you know how mud completely ruins a good pair of heels. I had these lovely navy blue pumps that I bought at Macy's... oh, they must be twenty years old now, but they still look brand new because I only wear them on special occasions. \n\nBut what were we... oh, getting up. No, no, I stay right there in my pew. I might be tapping my foot or fixing the pleats in my skirt, like I mentioned before, but I know better than to just stand up and walk out in the middle of a service or a meeting. I just stay put until it's time to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties are affecting your overall well-being or happiness?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I mean, nobody likes feeling like they can't keep their own life straight, do they? It does get me down sometimes. Arthur tells me I shouldn't fret so much, but he's always been the easygoing one. He's got this old armchair in the den, the brown leather one, and he can just sit there for hours reading his historical biographies. He's reading one right now about Abraham Lincoln. Did you know Lincoln kept important letters in his stovepipe hat? I always thought that was just a story they told children in school, but Arthur says it's right there in the book. It makes you wonder how he kept them from getting all sweaty in the summer, doesn't it? Summers back then must have been dreadful without air conditioning. We had our AC go out two years ago right in the middle of August, and it took the repairman three days to come out. Three days! We had fans running in every room, but it just blew the hot air around like a convection oven. \n\nBut anyway... what was I saying? Oh, my happiness. I don't know if I'd say it's affecting my overall happiness, exactly. I have a good life, a wonderful husband, beautiful grandchildren... I just wish I didn't feel so scattered all the time. It's frustrating, more than anything. Like I'm always trying to catch up to myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness at work or when you're alone, or are they mostly happening at home or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Well, since I'm retired, I don't really have a job to go to anymore. I used to work as a receptionist at a dental office, Dr. Peterson's office, right off Main Street. He was such a gentle dentist, never made you feel a thing when he gave those novocaine shots. He retired to Florida, Naples I think, or maybe it was Fort Myers. They get terrible hurricanes down there, don't they? I always worry about my sister, she lives near Tampa. She has this beautiful little lanai where she grows orchids, just stunning things. I tried to grow an orchid once, but I think I overwatered it. The leaves went all yellow and mushy. They say you're only supposed to give them an ice cube a week, which just sounds so strange for a tropical plant, doesn't it? Ice cubes in the jungle! \n\nBut... oh, where it happens. Yes. It's mostly at home, and over at the community center where I volunteer. It's actually started causing some real strain, to be honest. Arthur gets so frustrated with me, and the other ladies at the center... well, they just can't count on me to keep the plans straight anymore. If I'm supposed to organize the bake sale or keep track of the donations, someone always has to check behind me to make sure I didn't mix up the dates or lose the sign-up sheet. It's embarrassing, really. I just can't seem to keep it all organized in my head without someone looking over my shoulder.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness when you're alone at home?\n\nPatient: When I'm alone? Oh, well... I suppose I haven't really paid much attention to that. When Arthur is out playing golf with his buddies—they play every Tuesday and Thursday morning, weather permitting, of course. They play at that course over by the new shopping center. The one with the big Target? I went in there the other day just to pick up some new hand towels for the guest bathroom, because the old ones were getting so frayed around the edges. I don't know why they make things so poorly these days. You used to buy a towel and it would last you thirty years! Anyway, I walked out of there with three new throw pillows, a bag of potting soil, and completely forgot the towels. \n\nBut... what was I saying? Oh, being alone. I don't know, really. I guess I'm always doing something, but I couldn't tell you if I'm more restless or not when nobody else is around. I just sort of putter about the house.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they are speaking?\n\nPatient: Well, Arthur certainly thinks I do. He's always telling me I cut him off before he finishes his sentences. It drives him up the wall, especially when he's trying to tell one of his long golf stories. He has this one story about getting a hole-in-one on the 14th hole back in 1998, and he tells it with all these dramatic pauses. The 14th hole at that course has this awful water hazard right in front of the green, just a magnet for golf balls. My brother-in-law, Frank, lost three balls in that same pond on Thanksgiving morning once. We had to hold dinner for an hour because he refused to leave until he fished them out with that little scooper thing on a stick. By the time he got back, the turkey was practically dry as a bone. I had to make extra gravy just to save the meal, and you know how tricky gravy can be. You have to whisk it constantly or it gets all lumpy, and nobody likes lumpy gravy. \n\nBut... oh, interrupting. Yes, I do the same thing at the community center during our group planning meetings for the bake sales and things. I just... I get so afraid I'll lose the thought if I don't say it right then and there. It's like my brain is a leaky bucket, and if I don't pour the idea out immediately, it just drips away before I can share it. So I just blurt it out, even if someone else is talking. I don't mean to be rude, really I don't.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blur out answers before the questions have been completed when you're in a conversation?\n\nPatient: Blurting out answers? Oh, I don't know if I do that exactly. I mean, I do interrupt, like I said with Arthur and his golf stories. He really does take forever to get to the point. His brother Frank is the exact same way. They both get that from their mother, rest her soul. She could talk for forty-five minutes about the price of a head of lettuce at the Piggly Wiggly. She used to make this wonderful iceberg wedge salad with homemade blue cheese dressing. You can't find good blue cheese anymore, it's all that bottled stuff that tastes like vinegar and sadness. I tried making it from scratch once, but the cheese got all crumbly and wouldn't mix right with the mayonnaise. You really need a good quality mayonnaise for that, none of that low-fat nonsense. \n\nBut answering before someone finishes asking... I don't really think I do that. At least, nobody has ever pointed it out to me like they do with the interrupting. I just try to wait until they ask, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these feelings of restlessness and difficulty focusing when you're in quiet, low-stimulation environments, like when you're reading a book or taking a walk alone?\n\nPatient: Quiet things? Oh, I suppose they are rather difficult for me. Even when I try to do something peaceful, like my knitting... I joined a knitting circle at the library on Wednesday afternoons. They have this lovely room with big windows that look out over the community garden. The tomatoes they grew last summer were just spectacular. I tried growing tomatoes once, those little cherry ones, but the squirrels got to every single one of them before they even turned red. They're such menaces, those squirrels. They dug up all my tulip bulbs last fall, too. I had ordered these special black tulips from a catalog—well, they call them black, but they're really a very deep purple. They would have looked so striking next to the yellow daffodils. \n\nBut what was I... oh, the knitting. Yes, even when I'm just sitting there trying to knit quietly, I can't seem to just *be* quiet. I end up chatting with the woman next to me, or I start humming along to whatever tune is stuck in my head. Or I'll suddenly realize I need a different size needle and I have to get up and dig through my bag, and then I'll decide to organize my yarn while I'm at it. I just can't seem to sit calmly and do the quiet thing, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Waiting my turn? Oh, heavens, yes. It's dreadful. Like waiting in line at the pharmacy... I just get so fidgety, shifting from one foot to the other. The pharmacy down on Elm Street is always so backed up on Tuesday mornings. That's when they get their deliveries, you see, and the pharmacist—a lovely young man named David, he always wears these brightly colored bow ties—he gets so flustered trying to sort the new stock and help the customers. His wife just had a baby, a little girl. They named her Eleanor. Such a classic name, isn't it? Much better than these modern names people make up nowadays. My neighbor's daughter just named her baby \"Riot.\" Can you imagine? A little boy named Riot. I suppose it's fitting, though, he cries constantly. I can hear him through the living room wall when I'm trying to watch my afternoon programs. \n\nBut... oh, waiting. Yes. Whether it's the pharmacy line or waiting my turn to speak at those community center meetings, I just can't stand it. I catch myself sighing loudly, or I end up just jumping in too soon because I simply can't hold it in any longer. It's terribly impatient of me, I know.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I mean, nobody likes feeling like they can't keep their own life straight, do they? It does get me down sometimes. Arthur tells me I shouldn't fret so much, but he's always been the easygoing one. He's got this old armchair in the den, the brown leather one, and he can just sit there for hours reading his historical biographies. He's reading one right now about Abraham Lincoln. Did you know Lincoln kept important letters in his stovepipe hat? I always thought that was just a story they told children in school, but Arthur says it's right there in the book. It makes you wonder how he kept them from getting all sweaty in the summer, doesn't it? Summers back then must have been dreadful without air conditioning. We had our AC go out two years ago right in the middle of August, and it took the repairman three days to come out. Three days! We had fans running in every room, but it just blew the hot air around like a convection oven. \n\nBut anyway... what was I saying? Oh, my happiness. I don't know if I'd say it's affecting my overall happiness, exactly. I have a good life, a wonderful husband, beautiful grandchildren... I just wish I didn't feel so scattered all the time. It's frustrating, more than anything. Like I'm always trying to catch up to myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness at work or when you're alone, or are they mostly happening at home or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Well, since I'm retired, I don't really have a job to go to anymore. I used to work as a receptionist at a dental office, Dr. Peterson's office, right off Main Street. He was such a gentle dentist, never made you feel a thing when he gave those novocaine shots. He retired to Florida, Naples I think, or maybe it was Fort Myers. They get terrible hurricanes down there, don't they? I always worry about my sister, she lives near Tampa. She has this beautiful little lanai where she grows orchids, just stunning things. I tried to grow an orchid once, but I think I overwatered it. The leaves went all yellow and mushy. They say you're only supposed to give them an ice cube a week, which just sounds so strange for a tropical plant, doesn't it? Ice cubes in the jungle! \n\nBut... oh, where it happens. Yes. It's mostly at home, and over at the community center where I volunteer. It's actually started causing some real strain, to be honest. Arthur gets so frustrated with me, and the other ladies at the center... well, they just can't count on me to keep the plans straight anymore. If I'm supposed to organize the bake sale or keep track of the donations, someone always has to check behind me to make sure I didn't mix up the dates or lose the sign-up sheet. It's embarrassing, really. I just can't seem to keep it all organized in my head without someone looking over my shoulder.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness when you're alone at home?\n\nPatient: When I'm alone? Oh, well... I suppose I haven't really paid much attention to that. When Arthur is out playing golf with his buddies—they play every Tuesday and Thursday morning, weather permitting, of course. They play at that course over by the new shopping center. The one with the big Target? I went in there the other day just to pick up some new hand towels for the guest bathroom, because the old ones were getting so frayed around the edges. I don't know why they make things so poorly these days. You used to buy a towel and it would last you thirty years! Anyway, I walked out of there with three new throw pillows, a bag of potting soil, and completely forgot the towels. \n\nBut... what was I saying? Oh, being alone. I don't know, really. I guess I'm always doing something, but I couldn't tell you if I'm more restless or not when nobody else is around. I just sort of putter about the house.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they are speaking?\n\nPatient: Well, Arthur certainly thinks I do. He's always telling me I cut him off before he finishes his sentences. It drives him up the wall, especially when he's trying to tell one of his long golf stories. He has this one story about getting a hole-in-one on the 14th hole back in 1998, and he tells it with all these dramatic pauses. The 14th hole at that course has this awful water hazard right in front of the green, just a magnet for golf balls. My brother-in-law, Frank, lost three balls in that same pond on Thanksgiving morning once. We had to hold dinner for an hour because he refused to leave until he fished them out with that little scooper thing on a stick. By the time he got back, the turkey was practically dry as a bone. I had to make extra gravy just to save the meal, and you know how tricky gravy can be. You have to whisk it constantly or it gets all lumpy, and nobody likes lumpy gravy. \n\nBut... oh, interrupting. Yes, I do the same thing at the community center during our group planning meetings for the bake sales and things. I just... I get so afraid I'll lose the thought if I don't say it right then and there. It's like my brain is a leaky bucket, and if I don't pour the idea out immediately, it just drips away before I can share it. So I just blurt it out, even if someone else is talking. I don't mean to be rude, really I don't.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blur out answers before the questions have been completed when you're in a conversation?\n\nPatient: Blurting out answers? Oh, I don't know if I do that exactly. I mean, I do interrupt, like I said with Arthur and his golf stories. He really does take forever to get to the point. His brother Frank is the exact same way. They both get that from their mother, rest her soul. She could talk for forty-five minutes about the price of a head of lettuce at the Piggly Wiggly. She used to make this wonderful iceberg wedge salad with homemade blue cheese dressing. You can't find good blue cheese anymore, it's all that bottled stuff that tastes like vinegar and sadness. I tried making it from scratch once, but the cheese got all crumbly and wouldn't mix right with the mayonnaise. You really need a good quality mayonnaise for that, none of that low-fat nonsense. \n\nBut answering before someone finishes asking... I don't really think I do that. At least, nobody has ever pointed it out to me like they do with the interrupting. I just try to wait until they ask, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these feelings of restlessness and difficulty focusing when you're in quiet, low-stimulation environments, like when you're reading a book or taking a walk alone?\n\nPatient: Quiet things? Oh, I suppose they are rather difficult for me. Even when I try to do something peaceful, like my knitting... I joined a knitting circle at the library on Wednesday afternoons. They have this lovely room with big windows that look out over the community garden. The tomatoes they grew last summer were just spectacular. I tried growing tomatoes once, those little cherry ones, but the squirrels got to every single one of them before they even turned red. They're such menaces, those squirrels. They dug up all my tulip bulbs last fall, too. I had ordered these special black tulips from a catalog—well, they call them black, but they're really a very deep purple. They would have looked so striking next to the yellow daffodils. \n\nBut what was I... oh, the knitting. Yes, even when I'm just sitting there trying to knit quietly, I can't seem to just *be* quiet. I end up chatting with the woman next to me, or I start humming along to whatever tune is stuck in my head. Or I'll suddenly realize I need a different size needle and I have to get up and dig through my bag, and then I'll decide to organize my yarn while I'm at it. I just can't seem to sit calmly and do the quiet thing, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Waiting my turn? Oh, heavens, yes. It's dreadful. Like waiting in line at the pharmacy... I just get so fidgety, shifting from one foot to the other. The pharmacy down on Elm Street is always so backed up on Tuesday mornings. That's when they get their deliveries, you see, and the pharmacist—a lovely young man named David, he always wears these brightly colored bow ties—he gets so flustered trying to sort the new stock and help the customers. His wife just had a baby, a little girl. They named her Eleanor. Such a classic name, isn't it? Much better than these modern names people make up nowadays. My neighbor's daughter just named her baby \"Riot.\" Can you imagine? A little boy named Riot. I suppose it's fitting, though, he cries constantly. I can hear him through the living room wall when I'm trying to watch my afternoon programs. \n\nBut... oh, waiting. Yes. Whether it's the pharmacy line or waiting my turn to speak at those community center meetings, I just can't stand it. I catch myself sighing loudly, or I end up just jumping in too soon because I simply can't hold it in any longer. It's terribly impatient of me, I know.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Since I was a child? Oh, well... yes, I suppose they have been. I remember even back in grade school, my teachers were always telling my mother that I was a bright girl, but just so scattered. I was always losing my papers, or talking out of turn, and wiggling in my seat before my work was done. My third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable, she was a formidable woman. She wore these thick glasses on a little pearl chain around her neck, and she had this habit of tapping her ruler on the chalkboard when she wanted us to pay attention. She used a wooden ruler, not those plastic ones they have now. Plastic just doesn't have the same authoritative *smack*, does it? And the chalk! Oh, the dust was everywhere. They don't even use chalkboards anymore, do they? It's all those whiteboards with the markers that smell so strongly of alcohol. My grandson's school has smartboards, which are basically giant televisions. I don't know how children are supposed to learn anything with a television at the front of the room. It just seems like a distraction waiting to happen. I remember when television first became popular, my father refused to get one. He said it would rot our brains. He was a very strict man, my father. Worked at the post office for forty years. 

But... oh, what was I saying? Right, my teachers. Yes, they always said I was scattered and couldn't sit still. So, yes, it's been going on for a very long time.

Like, even on a quiet day, I’m bouncing from the garden club list to my knitting bag to my travel papers before I’ve really finished any one thing. \n\nAnd speaking of the garden club, we're trying to organize the spring tulip sale right now, and let me tell you, getting anyone to volunteer for the morning shift is just like pulling teeth. I just never seem to settle down and finish one project before I'm up and on to the next one.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself struggling to complete tasks because you get distracted or lose focus?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. Like with the church bulletin or when we have our planning meetings for the garden club. I can start out listening just fine, really paying attention, but then after a little while, I realize I’ve completely missed half of what was said or printed. It’s like my mind just wanders off. \n\nJust last week at the meeting, Pastor Dave was talking about the new choir robes, and I was looking at the bulletin, and suddenly I was thinking about how the choir sounded so lovely on Easter Sunday. My granddaughter sang a solo, you know. She has the voice of an angel, really she does. She's taking lessons from Mrs. Higgins down the street, who used to sing in the opera, or so she says, though I’ve never actually seen any proof of that. But anyway, by the time I tuned back into the meeting, they were already voting on the budget for the robes, and I had no earthly idea what the numbers were! So yes, I do lose focus quite a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your inability to focus and complete tasks is causing problems for you in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, I hate to admit it, but yes, it really is causing some trouble lately. It’s starting to put a real strain on things at home, and over at the community center, too. My husband, Arthur—bless his heart, he’s a patient man, usually—but he’s getting so frustrated because he feels like he can’t count on me to keep our plans straight. Just yesterday, we were supposed to go to the hardware store for some new hinges for the back gate. The old ones are rusting terribly, you know, because of all that rain I mentioned. We’ve had the worst luck with rust on that side of the house. I think it’s because the gutters over there always overflow when it pours. Arthur’s been meaning to clean them out since October. \n\nBut anyway, what was it? Oh, yes, the plans. He says he always has to check behind me now to make sure I’ve actually written things down or finished what I said I would. And the other volunteers at the center are starting to do the same thing. It makes me feel awful, really, like I'm a burden, but I just... I get so easily sidetracked.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Well, act on impulse? I don't know if I'd say that, exactly. I mean, I don't go out and buy a new car on a whim or anything like that. Though, speaking of cars, Arthur did just get the oil changed in the Buick. He always takes it to that little shop on Elm Street. The mechanic there, Joe, he’s such a nice young man. He always asks about my granddaughter. He actually went to high school with her mother, my daughter Susan. Susan was a cheerleader, you know, and she had the most beautiful uniform. It was blue and gold, and I had to wash it by hand every Sunday night so it would be ready for the game on Monday. We used to use that special soap... oh, what was it called? It came in a pink box. \n\nBut anyway, impulse... no, I don't think I do things without thinking. I just get distracted, like I said. I might start something and then see something else that needs doing, but I don't think I'm impulsive. Just... busy, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus and completing tasks are affecting your relationships with friends or your role in the community?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was mentioning just a minute ago, yes, it really is. It's causing a real strain, both at home with Arthur and over at the community center. The other volunteers—especially Martha and Susan... oh, not my daughter Susan, a different Susan. Susan Miller. She makes the most wonderful peach cobbler for our charity bake sales. We had a sale just last month to raise money for the new library books, and her cobbler sold out in ten minutes! I tried to get the recipe from her, but she says it's a strict family secret passed down from her great-grandmother who lived in Georgia. They really do grow the best peaches down in Georgia, don't they? Arthur and I took a road trip down there back in '98... or maybe it was '99? We drove the whole way in that old station wagon we used to have. \n\nBut anyway, what was I... oh, right, the center. Yes, Susan and the other ladies, they feel like they just can't count on me to keep our plans straight anymore. They're always having to check behind me now, just like Arthur does, to make sure I actually finished setting up the tables or calling the folks I was supposed to call. So yes, it’s definitely affecting my role there. It makes me feel just awful that they have to baby me like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Well, restless... I suppose you could say that. Arthur always tells me I'm like a little motor that won't quite shut off. Especially when we're just sitting in the evenings watching television. We like to watch Jeopardy and Wheel of Fortune right after dinner. Pat Sajak, you know, I heard he was retiring. Or maybe he already did? Vanna White is still there, though, bless her. She wears the most gorgeous gowns. I actually tried to sew a dress like one of hers for my niece's wedding years ago—oh, what a disaster that was. The silk just kept slipping right under the presser foot of my old Singer machine. I’ve had that sewing machine since 1974, can you believe it? My mother bought it for me at Sears. \n\nBut what was I... oh, sitting still. Yes. When I’m watching television, or even if I'm just sitting in a meeting for the neighborhood watch, I just can't seem to stay entirely still. I’m usually tapping my foot on the rug, or rubbing my fingers together, or just fussing with whatever happens to be in my lap. A napkin, the remote control, the buttons on my cardigan... I just always seem to be fiddling with something. I don't even realize I'm doing it half the time until Arthur reaches over and pats my hand to make me stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself leaving your seat when you're supposed to be sitting still?\n\nPatient: Leave my seat? Oh, heavens no. I don't just get up and wander off when I'm supposed to be sitting. Well, unless I'm at home and remember I left the kettle on, but that's different. Now, when we're at church on Sunday mornings, for instance... Pastor Thomas has been giving these very long sermons lately. He’s a wonderful speaker, truly he is, but he does tend to go on a bit. His wife, Mary, she plays the organ, and she has the most beautiful singing voice. She actually sang at my niece's wedding—the same one I tried to make that silk dress for! It was an outdoor wedding, out by the lake, and the weather was just perfect. Not a single cloud in the sky. We were all so worried it would rain because it had just poured the entire week before, and you know how mud completely ruins a good pair of heels. I had these lovely navy blue pumps that I bought at Macy's... oh, they must be twenty years old now, but they still look brand new because I only wear them on special occasions. \n\nBut what were we... oh, getting up. No, no, I stay right there in my pew. I might be tapping my foot or fixing the pleats in my skirt, like I mentioned before, but I know better than to just stand up and walk out in the middle of a service or a meeting. I just stay put until it's time to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties are affecting your overall well-being or happiness?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I mean, nobody likes feeling like they can't keep their own life straight, do they? It does get me down sometimes. Arthur tells me I shouldn't fret so much, but he's always been the easygoing one. He's got this old armchair in the den, the brown leather one, and he can just sit there for hours reading his historical biographies. He's reading one right now about Abraham Lincoln. Did you know Lincoln kept important letters in his stovepipe hat? I always thought that was just a story they told children in school, but Arthur says it's right there in the book. It makes you wonder how he kept them from getting all sweaty in the summer, doesn't it? Summers back then must have been dreadful without air conditioning. We had our AC go out two years ago right in the middle of August, and it took the repairman three days to come out. Three days! We had fans running in every room, but it just blew the hot air around like a convection oven. \n\nBut anyway... what was I saying? Oh, my happiness. I don't know if I'd say it's affecting my overall happiness, exactly. I have a good life, a wonderful husband, beautiful grandchildren... I just wish I didn't feel so scattered all the time. It's frustrating, more than anything. Like I'm always trying to catch up to myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness at work or when you're alone, or are they mostly happening at home or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Well, since I'm retired, I don't really have a job to go to anymore. I used to work as a receptionist at a dental office, Dr. Peterson's office, right off Main Street. He was such a gentle dentist, never made you feel a thing when he gave those novocaine shots. He retired to Florida, Naples I think, or maybe it was Fort Myers. They get terrible hurricanes down there, don't they? I always worry about my sister, she lives near Tampa. She has this beautiful little lanai where she grows orchids, just stunning things. I tried to grow an orchid once, but I think I overwatered it. The leaves went all yellow and mushy. They say you're only supposed to give them an ice cube a week, which just sounds so strange for a tropical plant, doesn't it? Ice cubes in the jungle! \n\nBut... oh, where it happens. Yes. It's mostly at home, and over at the community center where I volunteer. It's actually started causing some real strain, to be honest. Arthur gets so frustrated with me, and the other ladies at the center... well, they just can't count on me to keep the plans straight anymore. If I'm supposed to organize the bake sale or keep track of the donations, someone always has to check behind me to make sure I didn't mix up the dates or lose the sign-up sheet. It's embarrassing, really. I just can't seem to keep it all organized in my head without someone looking over my shoulder.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness when you're alone at home?\n\nPatient: When I'm alone? Oh, well... I suppose I haven't really paid much attention to that. When Arthur is out playing golf with his buddies—they play every Tuesday and Thursday morning, weather permitting, of course. They play at that course over by the new shopping center. The one with the big Target? I went in there the other day just to pick up some new hand towels for the guest bathroom, because the old ones were getting so frayed around the edges. I don't know why they make things so poorly these days. You used to buy a towel and it would last you thirty years! Anyway, I walked out of there with three new throw pillows, a bag of potting soil, and completely forgot the towels. \n\nBut... what was I saying? Oh, being alone. I don't know, really. I guess I'm always doing something, but I couldn't tell you if I'm more restless or not when nobody else is around. I just sort of putter about the house.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they are speaking?\n\nPatient: Well, Arthur certainly thinks I do. He's always telling me I cut him off before he finishes his sentences. It drives him up the wall, especially when he's trying to tell one of his long golf stories. He has this one story about getting a hole-in-one on the 14th hole back in 1998, and he tells it with all these dramatic pauses. The 14th hole at that course has this awful water hazard right in front of the green, just a magnet for golf balls. My brother-in-law, Frank, lost three balls in that same pond on Thanksgiving morning once. We had to hold dinner for an hour because he refused to leave until he fished them out with that little scooper thing on a stick. By the time he got back, the turkey was practically dry as a bone. I had to make extra gravy just to save the meal, and you know how tricky gravy can be. You have to whisk it constantly or it gets all lumpy, and nobody likes lumpy gravy. \n\nBut... oh, interrupting. Yes, I do the same thing at the community center during our group planning meetings for the bake sales and things. I just... I get so afraid I'll lose the thought if I don't say it right then and there. It's like my brain is a leaky bucket, and if I don't pour the idea out immediately, it just drips away before I can share it. So I just blurt it out, even if someone else is talking. I don't mean to be rude, really I don't.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blur out answers before the questions have been completed when you're in a conversation?\n\nPatient: Blurting out answers? Oh, I don't know if I do that exactly. I mean, I do interrupt, like I said with Arthur and his golf stories. He really does take forever to get to the point. His brother Frank is the exact same way. They both get that from their mother, rest her soul. She could talk for forty-five minutes about the price of a head of lettuce at the Piggly Wiggly. She used to make this wonderful iceberg wedge salad with homemade blue cheese dressing. You can't find good blue cheese anymore, it's all that bottled stuff that tastes like vinegar and sadness. I tried making it from scratch once, but the cheese got all crumbly and wouldn't mix right with the mayonnaise. You really need a good quality mayonnaise for that, none of that low-fat nonsense. \n\nBut answering before someone finishes asking... I don't really think I do that. At least, nobody has ever pointed it out to me like they do with the interrupting. I just try to wait until they ask, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these feelings of restlessness and difficulty focusing when you're in quiet, low-stimulation environments, like when you're reading a book or taking a walk alone?\n\nPatient: Quiet things? Oh, I suppose they are rather difficult for me. Even when I try to do something peaceful, like my knitting... I joined a knitting circle at the library on Wednesday afternoons. They have this lovely room with big windows that look out over the community garden. The tomatoes they grew last summer were just spectacular. I tried growing tomatoes once, those little cherry ones, but the squirrels got to every single one of them before they even turned red. They're such menaces, those squirrels. They dug up all my tulip bulbs last fall, too. I had ordered these special black tulips from a catalog—well, they call them black, but they're really a very deep purple. They would have looked so striking next to the yellow daffodils. \n\nBut what was I... oh, the knitting. Yes, even when I'm just sitting there trying to knit quietly, I can't seem to just *be* quiet. I end up chatting with the woman next to me, or I start humming along to whatever tune is stuck in my head. Or I'll suddenly realize I need a different size needle and I have to get up and dig through my bag, and then I'll decide to organize my yarn while I'm at it. I just can't seem to sit calmly and do the quiet thing, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Waiting my turn? Oh, heavens, yes. It's dreadful. Like waiting in line at the pharmacy... I just get so fidgety, shifting from one foot to the other. The pharmacy down on Elm Street is always so backed up on Tuesday mornings. That's when they get their deliveries, you see, and the pharmacist—a lovely young man named David, he always wears these brightly colored bow ties—he gets so flustered trying to sort the new stock and help the customers. His wife just had a baby, a little girl. They named her Eleanor. Such a classic name, isn't it? Much better than these modern names people make up nowadays. My neighbor's daughter just named her baby \"Riot.\" Can you imagine? A little boy named Riot. I suppose it's fitting, though, he cries constantly. I can hear him through the living room wall when I'm trying to watch my afternoon programs. \n\nBut... oh, waiting. Yes. Whether it's the pharmacy line or waiting my turn to speak at those community center meetings, I just can't stand it. I catch myself sighing loudly, or I end up just jumping in too soon because I simply can't hold it in any longer. It's terribly impatient of me, I know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and interrupting others have been present since you were a child, or did they develop later in life?\n\nPatient: Since I was a child? Oh, well... yes, I suppose they have been. I remember even back in grade school, my teachers were always telling my mother that I was a bright girl, but just so scattered. I was always losing my papers, or talking out of turn, and wiggling in my seat before my work was done. My third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable, she was a formidable woman. She wore these thick glasses on a little pearl chain around her neck, and she had this habit of tapping her ruler on the chalkboard when she wanted us to pay attention. She used a wooden ruler, not those plastic ones they have now. Plastic just doesn't have the same authoritative *smack*, does it? And the chalk! Oh, the dust was everywhere. They don't even use chalkboards anymore, do they? It's all those whiteboards with the markers that smell so strongly of alcohol. My grandson's school has smartboards, which are basically giant televisions. I don't know how children are supposed to learn anything with a television at the front of the room. It just seems like a distraction waiting to happen. I remember when television first became popular, my father refused to get one. He said it would rot our brains. He was a very strict man, my father. Worked at the post office for forty years. \n\nBut... oh, what was I saying? Right, my teachers. Yes, they always said I was scattered and couldn't sit still. So, yes, it's been going on for a very long time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It's like my brain is a leaky bucket, and if I don't pour the idea out immediately, it just drips away before I can share it. So I just blurt it out, even if someone else is talking. I don't mean to be rude, really I don't.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blur out answers before the questions have been completed when you're in a conversation?\n\nPatient: Blurting out answers? Oh, I don't know if I do that exactly. I mean, I do interrupt, like I said with Arthur and his golf stories. He really does take forever to get to the point. His brother Frank is the exact same way. They both get that from their mother, rest her soul. She could talk for forty-five minutes about the price of a head of lettuce at the Piggly Wiggly. She used to make this wonderful iceberg wedge salad with homemade blue cheese dressing. You can't find good blue cheese anymore, it's all that bottled stuff that tastes like vinegar and sadness. I tried making it from scratch once, but the cheese got all crumbly and wouldn't mix right with the mayonnaise. You really need a good quality mayonnaise for that, none of that low-fat nonsense. \n\nBut answering before someone finishes asking... I don't really think I do that. At least, nobody has ever pointed it out to me like they do with the interrupting. I just try to wait until they ask, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these feelings of restlessness and difficulty focusing when you're in quiet, low-stimulation environments, like when you're reading a book or taking a walk alone?\n\nPatient: Quiet things? Oh, I suppose they are rather difficult for me. Even when I try to do something peaceful, like my knitting... I joined a knitting circle at the library on Wednesday afternoons. They have this lovely room with big windows that look out over the community garden. The tomatoes they grew last summer were just spectacular. I tried growing tomatoes once, those little cherry ones, but the squirrels got to every single one of them before they even turned red. They're such menaces, those squirrels. They dug up all my tulip bulbs last fall, too. I had ordered these special black tulips from a catalog—well, they call them black, but they're really a very deep purple. They would have looked so striking next to the yellow daffodils. \n\nBut what was I... oh, the knitting. Yes, even when I'm just sitting there trying to knit quietly, I can't seem to just *be* quiet. I end up chatting with the woman next to me, or I start humming along to whatever tune is stuck in my head. Or I'll suddenly realize I need a different size needle and I have to get up and dig through my bag, and then I'll decide to organize my yarn while I'm at it. I just can't seem to sit calmly and do the quiet thing, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Waiting my turn? Oh, heavens, yes. It's dreadful. Like waiting in line at the pharmacy... I just get so fidgety, shifting from one foot to the other. The pharmacy down on Elm Street is always so backed up on Tuesday mornings. That's when they get their deliveries, you see, and the pharmacist—a lovely young man named David, he always wears these brightly colored bow ties—he gets so flustered trying to sort the new stock and help the customers. His wife just had a baby, a little girl. They named her Eleanor. Such a classic name, isn't it? Much better than these modern names people make up nowadays. My neighbor's daughter just named her baby \"Riot.\" Can you imagine? A little boy named Riot. I suppose it's fitting, though, he cries constantly. I can hear him through the living room wall when I'm trying to watch my afternoon programs. \n\nBut... oh, waiting. Yes. Whether it's the pharmacy line or waiting my turn to speak at those community center meetings, I just can't stand it. I catch myself sighing loudly, or I end up just jumping in too soon because I simply can't hold it in any longer. It's terribly impatient of me, I know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and interrupting others have been present since you were a child, or did they develop later in life?\n\nPatient: Since I was a child? Oh, well... yes, I suppose they have been. I remember even back in grade school, my teachers were always telling my mother that I was a bright girl, but just so scattered. I was always losing my papers, or talking out of turn, and wiggling in my seat before my work was done. My third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable, she was a formidable woman. She wore these thick glasses on a little pearl chain around her neck, and she had this habit of tapping her ruler on the chalkboard when she wanted us to pay attention. She used a wooden ruler, not those plastic ones they have now. Plastic just doesn't have the same authoritative *smack*, does it? And the chalk! Oh, the dust was everywhere. They don't even use chalkboards anymore, do they? It's all those whiteboards with the markers that smell so strongly of alcohol. My grandson's school has smartboards, which are basically giant televisions. I don't know how children are supposed to learn anything with a television at the front of the room. It just seems like a distraction waiting to happen. I remember when television first became popular, my father refused to get one. He said it would rot our brains. He was a very strict man, my father. Worked at the post office for forty years. \n\nBut... oh, what was I saying? Right, my teachers. Yes, they always said I was scattered and couldn't sit still. So, yes, it's been going on for a very long time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Distress? Well... yes, I suppose it has. It’s been causing some real strain lately, especially at home and down at the community center. My husband, Arthur—he’s usually so patient, bless him—but even he’s getting frustrated because he just can’t count on me to keep our plans straight. Just last week, we were supposed to go to his sister’s house for Sunday dinner. She makes this incredible pot roast. I don’t know what she puts in the gravy, but it’s just rich and savory. I’ve asked for the recipe a dozen times, but she always changes the subject. I think she uses a splash of red wine, maybe a Merlot. We went to a vineyard once, Arthur and I, for our twenty-fifth anniversary. Up in upstate New York. The leaves were turning, all those brilliant reds and oranges. We stayed at this little bed and breakfast run by a woman who collected antique teapots. They were lined up on every available surface. I was terrified I’d knock one over with my purse. I have a rather large purse, you see, because I need to carry my knitting, and my glasses, and usually a paperback... 

Oh, but what was I... right, the strain. Yes, Arthur gets upset because I forgot to write down the time for the dinner, and we ended up arriving an hour late. And it’s the same at the community center. I volunteer for the bake sale committee, but the other ladies have started double-checking everything I do. If I say I’m bringing the napkins, someone else brings a backup pack just in case I forget. It’s a bit embarrassing, really, knowing they feel they have to check behind me all the time. So, yes, it does cause some trouble for me.

He always takes it to that little shop on Elm Street. The mechanic there, Joe, he’s such a nice young man. He always asks about my granddaughter. He actually went to high school with her mother, my daughter Susan. Susan was a cheerleader, you know, and she had the most beautiful uniform. It was blue and gold, and I had to wash it by hand every Sunday night so it would be ready for the game on Monday. We used to use that special soap... oh, what was it called?
